However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The recently renovated Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort offers great views, a well-equipped fitness center and delicious food.

Hilton is making a big play in South Florida with its Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort. The massive 1,000-room property — which underwent an $80 million renovation before opening under the Signia Hilton brand in May 2026 — sits on the beach in Hollywood, Florida, just outside of Fort Lauderdale.

The resort caters primarily to business and group travelers (it’s attached to a massive events space), but it’s also a strong option for vacationers looking for some R&R. The main downside: The location isn’t great if your trip primarily revolves around off-property excursions, dining and nightlife.

Room rates typically run $300 to $600 a night, depending on the season, but you can also use Hilton Honors benefits to make your stay more affordable. There’s also a resort fee of $59 per night — but Hilton waives that when you book using Hilton points.

Hilton covered the cost of my stay for two nights, but the opinions in this review are my own. Here’s what guests can expect at this oceanfront resort.

Since the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort is part of Hilton Honors, there are several ways to book a stay there.

If you’re using Hilton points, expect to pay 70,000 to 80,000 points a night when standard rooms are available. Hilton elite status — a benefit you can earn by holding any Hilton Honors credit card — gets you the fifth-night-free perk, where you stay for at least five nights but pay for four when booking with points.

For example, if you were to stay five nights at 80,000 points a night, you’d pay 320,000 points total, bringing down your average nightly rate to 64,000 points.

You can also book using Hilton’s generous free night certificates, which can be used at almost any property in the portfolio. If you hold the Hilton Honors American Express Aspire Card, you automatically get a certificate each year. It’s also possible to earn certificates through reaching spending thresholds on the card and on the Hilton Honors American Express Surpass® Card. Terms apply.

Make sure to check the cash rate against the points rate to make sure you’re getting good value from your redemption. NerdWallet values Hilton points at 0.4 cent apiece.

For cash bookings of two nights or more, you might consider using The Hotel Collection via American Express’ travel portal for eligible cardholders. Eligible bookings come with benefits like a $100 property credit and other amenities subject to availability, including a room upgrade, early check-in and late checkout.

Keep in mind that third-party bookings typically don’t earn Hilton points or count toward elite status qualification.

It’s hard to miss the Signia by Hilton Diplomat Beach Resort, with its signature H-shaped facade and sleek white-and-glass exterior.

Stepping into the hotel, I was immediately struck by the lush greenery filling the entrance and indoor courtyard. The grand lobby has soaring glass ceilings, giving the space a greenhouse feel, although the strong air conditioning keeps you cool.

Despite its massive size, it doesn’t have a sprawling layout, which I appreciated. Everything is close together, and you can walk from the check-in counter to any of the indoor restaurants in under a minute.

I stayed in an oceanfront suite with a balcony, which was spacious and had a fun beachy vibe. To get to the rooms, you’ll need to use either the north or south tower elevator banks.

In the living room, there was a large sitting area featuring a TV, a couch, an accent chair and a four-person high-top table.

Although I didn’t spend too much time on the balcony — the October balminess was a bit too much for me — I enjoyed stepping out each morning to admire the view.

The bedroom had a king bed with extremely comfortable bedding and an oversized chair that was a great spot to relax and read a book by the window.

The bathroom was spacious, featuring a shower and a separate tub (something every room has).

Even the standard room I toured felt roomy. I liked that it shared similar elements with the suite, including the oversized lounge chair.

This is what you’d normally get when booking a room at the cheapest points price.

There are several dining venues on the property, ranging from a grab-and-go cafe to a high-end steakhouse. It is a luxury resort, so expect the prices to reflect that.

For breakfast, you can head to Palmea Kitchen, which is also open for lunch. I had the breakfast buffet, which offered a nice spread of classics, from hot food items and pastries to cold cuts and smoked salmon.

If you don’t have time for a sit-down breakfast, you can head next door to The Press, which offers made-to-order and grab-and-go food options throughout the day. You can also get smoothies, juices, snacks, pastries and other sweet treats.

The Canteen is another grab-and-go option that also sells snacks, drinks, souvenirs and sundries, similar to a convenience store.

For lunch and dinner, try Solara. It doesn’t have a traditional restaurant feel, as it’s mostly an indoor-outdoor bar space. One side connects to the lobby, while the other side faces the Solara adults-only pool.

It serves a lunch menu, as well as a substantial bar food menu that’s available late into the night.

You can also eat at Playa Beach Bar & Grill, a beachside restaurant serving Latin-inspired cuisine. I enjoyed spending a lazy lunch there and watching the waves crash onto the shore.

For a fancy sit-down dinner, go to Diplomat Prime, the property’s steakhouse. We had a group dinner at the restaurant, and the meats were, of course, the highlight. But don’t sleep on the seafood, which was also delicious.

One of the best parts of the Dip, as it’s affectionately known, is its location right on the beach. Guests can access the stretch of beach directly adjacent to the property. But it’s not a private beach, so you might encounter crowds.

I didn’t spend any time on the beach itself — it was extremely windy while I was there — but the hotel’s resort fee includes amenities like a beach lounge (umbrella not included), a daily one-hour kayak or paddleboard rental, and beachfront yoga classes.

If you don’t want to get sand everywhere, you can head to one of the three pool options: the adults-only Solara pool, the family-friendly lagoon pool and the Verandah Social Club for those 21 and older (requires paid access).

The family-friendly lagoon spans the length of the property and runs beneath the Solara pool. It also has its own hot tub.

I spent an afternoon in a cabana at the Verandah Social Club, which has some smaller plunge pools and a hot tub.

I appreciated the attentive service from the staff there, who constantly checked on whether guests needed anything.

If you’re looking for other ways to stay active, head to the two-story fitness center, featuring a variety of equipment like free weights, exercise bikes, a rowing machine and treadmills.

Next door, you’ll find the spa and wellness center. I had a relaxing massage that incorporated hot stones and aromatherapy.

The locker rooms come with a sauna and steam room, although I wished there were more amenities like a hot tub and cold plunge.

One of the resort fee inclusions is complimentary high-intensity interval training workout classes at the gym across the street. I signed up for an early morning class. The instructor was friendly, and it was a great way to start my day.

Other amenities include a complimentary one-hour e-bike rental each day, as well as a kids’ club with various activities (but drop-off fees apply).

I had a fantastic stay at this resort, but I’d recommend it for a specific traveler.

If you’re planning to stay on-property your entire trip, it’s a great option. That’s probably why it’s such a popular convention hotel, since everything you need is easily accessible.

I loved the hotel’s design, including the jungle-like atmosphere in the lobby, and I found the dining options to be tasty, albeit pricey.

But if your trip revolves around visiting Miami attractions, you’ll likely want to stay closer to the action.

#3172. Is Coca-Cola a Millionaire-Maker Stock?

Sometimes boring stocks have the brightest futures.

By Will Ebiefung – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Coca-Cola ›

Few businesses exemplify American business success more than Coca-Cola (KO +0.94%). Since its founding in the late 19th century, the multinational beverage maker has expanded globally to serve billions and become one of the world's most recognized brands. Investors have profited substantially along the way, and this shows little sign of stopping.

Over the last 12 months alone, shares have jumped by an impressive 35%, substantially outpacing the S&P 500's return of just 8% over the same time frame. This is a big deal for a company that is already large and mature. Let's dig deeper into the pros and cons of Coca-Cola to decide if the company still has millionaire-maker potential.

To understand Coca-Cola's appeal, it can pay to listen to the philosophy of one of its biggest backers, Warren Buffett. His Berkshire Hathaway portfolio owns a whopping $38.4 billion worth of Coca-Cola stock, making it his third-largest bet behind American Express and Apple.

Buffett likes to invest in companies with simple and easy-to-understand business models that enjoy a strong economic moat. A moat is a competitive advantage that protects a company's market share against competition and allows it to enjoy stable or elevated pricing power. Coca-Cola is arguably the quintessential example of this type of business.

You can walk into a convenience store or restaurant practically anywhere in the world and find Coca-Cola's flagship beverage Coke (or one of its many subbrands like Sprite, Fanta, and a variety of localized options).

The company's ubiquitous shelf space has created unparalleled habit creation among its consumers, which is something new rivals will struggle to disrupt, even if they manage to create better-tasting products. This advantage also makes Coca-Cola's sales remarkably recession-resistant. It has allowed the company to protect its margins amid soaring global inflation in raw materials like aluminum and sugar.

Coca-Cola's gross margins have hovered around 60%, despite the soaring prices for its products. This suggests the company is passing on the vast majority of inflation to consumers, while protecting its own profitability.

Coca-Cola isn't without some long-term challenges. The company is already a global brand name, and that means there is very little room for organic growth. Management will be increasingly tempted to rely on price increases to juice investor returns. While the company's gross margin of over 60% was easy to justify back when a 12-pack cost $4.00, it is much harder to stomach when prices have doubled, and more American families are struggling to make ends meet.

Soda prices have far exceeded the U.S. inflation rate, despite boasting a disproportionately middle- and lower-income consumer base. It's hard not to wonder if this situation is as sustainable as it may look on the surface.

Over the long term, there could be a generational shift away from brand-name soft drinks as people become more comfortable buying lower-cost store brands from major retailers like Walmart, which offers alternatives like Sam's Cola and Twist Up.

Coca-Cola's recent performance is impressive, and its strong moat can help set the company up for more market-beating growth over the long term. That said, with a market cap of $422 billion (in a mature industry with relatively little room for organic growth), Coca-Cola's days of explosive millionaire-making growth are likely behind it. Shares are also somewhat expensive at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple of 28.

Investors who buy the stock now should look at it as a safer alternative to more speculative opportunities like generative AI. The company's dividend (currently boasting a yield of 2.21%) will likely represent a sizable percentage of its long-term returns.

#3171. Pfizer's Launched and Acquired Drugs Grew 32% in Q3 — and 3 Other Reasons to Buy the Stock Before 2027

Pfizer remains deeply unloved on Wall Street, but it is slowly turning a corner.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Pfizer (PFE -0.51%) faces a large patent cliff, and investors worry it won't be able to offset the revenue it loses as older drugs face generic competition. That's a problem, but the drugmaker is aware of it and is making important strides in its efforts to address the issue. The big story, however, is that this issue is pretty normal for a drug company.

In fact, revenue from recently launched and acquired drugs rose 32% in the third quarter of 2026. That shows the pharmaceutical giant's efforts are bearing fruit. But there's more reason to be excited, including the company's strong balance sheet, its large dividend, and management's commitment to continue paying that dividend. Here's what you need to know.

One of the biggest worries about Pfizer is its upcoming patent expirations. Two big ones are Eliquis in 2028 and Vyndamax in 2031. But patent expirations are a normal part of the pharma sector. And research and development efforts in the highly technical and intensely competitive healthcare sector don't always align perfectly with a company's patent expiration schedule. It's just a fact of life.

The 32% revenue growth Pfizer achieved from launched and acquired products is evidence that the company is seeing some success in its efforts to address the issue. And it sets up three more reasons to consider buying the stock.

Pfizer ended the third quarter of 2026 with $1.8 billion in cash and $12.4 billion in short-term investments. That gives the company significant firepower to support its business through this patent-expiration cycle. In fact, the company's cash hoard is slightly higher than at the start of the year, so it isn't rapidly burning through its savings.

Despite the company's results with new drugs and its financial strength, investors are deeply negative on Pfizer's stock. That's highlighted by its huge 6.1% dividend yield as of this writing. To put that yield into context, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, and the average pharma stock yields 1.3%.

The negativity highlighted by the elevated yield is echoed by more traditional valuation metrics. Pfizer's price-to-sales and price-to-book ratios are below their five-year averages. And while the price-to-earnings ratio is elevated, it is largely due to one-time charges. Looking at the whole picture, Pfizer's stock looks attractively priced.

Given the lofty yield, Pfizer is likely to be most appealing to dividend-focused investors. But right now earnings aren't covering the dividend. That's not a great sign, but one-time charges are part of the issue. If you look at cash flow, the company can still support the dividend. That's highlighted by a cash dividend payout ratio of roughly 85%. While a lower number would be better, there's still some room for adversity. And don't forget about the company's large cash pile.

Both of these facts help support management's strong backing of the dividend. Indeed, one of Pfizer's key capital priorities is to "maintain and grow our dividend." Obviously, the company's priorities could change, but it wouldn't make a statement like that if the board of directors wasn't on board with protecting the dividend.

At the end of the day, Pfizer is facing very real headwinds today. But it is an industry-leading company with a long history of success. If history is any guide, it will muddle through this difficult period. The success it is having with new drugs, its financial strength, attractive valuation, and dedication to its large dividend are all strong reasons for investors to take a deep dive before 2026 is over. If you wait too long, the opportunity here may pass you by.

Atmos Rewards offers six communities, each with its own travel perks and opportunities to earn bonus points

Atmos Rewards is the airline loyalty program for Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines. One of its newer features is Atmos Communities, which offer additional rewards and benefits tailored to different travel interests.

There are six communities, including two reserved for Alaska and Hawaii residents. The other four are open to members regardless of where they live. But don’t get caught up in the community names. The best choice for you may depend more on your travel plans than the groupings suggest.

Here’s how to decide which Atmos Community best fits your travel patterns.

Atmos Communities are like groups within the Atmos Rewards program that offer additional benefits based on different travel interests.

They’re free to join for Atmos Rewards members. Four communities are available to all members, while community Club 49 is reserved for Alaska residents and community Huaka’i by Hawaiian is reserved for Hawaii residents.

You can belong to only one community at a time and switch communities once per calendar year. Consider your upcoming travel plans before choosing.

Joining a community is easy: Log in to your Atmos Rewards account and navigate to “Account overview.” Select “Join a Community,” browse the options and click the “Join” button under the one you choose.

Each Atmos Community has a list of 10 destinations where members can earn bonus points and status points. Although the destinations differ by community, the rewards structure is the same.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, members can earn up to 10,000 bonus points and 10,000 status points by visiting qualifying destinations.

Here’s how many points you’ll earn as you reach each milestone:

Visit one destination, earn 100 status and bonus points.

Visit two destinations, earn 200 status and bonus points.

Visit three destinations, earn 300 status and bonus points.

Visit four destinations, earn 400 status and bonus points.

Visit five destinations, earn 500 status and bonus points.

Visit six destinations, earn 750 status and bonus points.

Visit seven destinations, earn 1,000 status and bonus points.

Visit eight destinations, earn 1,250 status and bonus points.

Visit nine destinations, earn 2,500 status and bonus points.

Visit 10 destinations, earn 3,000 status and bonus points.

The first member of each community to visit all 10 qualifying destinations during the challenge period will also earn 100,000 bonus points.

New lists of qualifying destinations for each community take effect on Jan. 1, 2028.

Here’s a closer look at the six communities, their benefits and the destinations eligible for bonus points.

Global Locals is geared toward frequent international travelers. Benefits include:

Earn 10% more status points toward Atmos elite status on flights that begin and/or end outside the U.S., including award flights.

Earn 1 status point for every 25 points transferred to Atmos Rewards from Bilt or Marriott Bonvoy.

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Through Dec. 31, 2027, these 10 Oneworld Alliance hubs count toward bonus points and status points. Qualifying layovers also count toward the destination challenge, so you don’t necessarily need to make these cities your final stop.

If you frequently travel internationally or have any upcoming flights through any of these airports, including qualifying layovers, Global Locals might be a fit for you.

If you have the Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® credit card, you’ll earn 3 Atmos points per $1 spent on eligible Alaska and Hawaiian Airlines purchases, dining and foreign transactions. The last bonus category can be especially useful for frequent international travelers.

This community has a similar setup to Global Locals, but most of its bonus destinations are in the U.S. Here’s what you can get:

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Families On the Go might appeal to you if you redeem Atmos Rewards for your children. Benefits include:

Get 20% of your points back on eligible award flights for kids 12 and under after you complete your trip.

Get 20% off Atmos Rewards Unlocked experiences.

Exclusively for Alaska residents, Club 49 offers rewards that are focused on travel to, from and within the state:

Two free checked bags when traveling to or from Alaska on Alaska Airlines or Hawaiian Airlines.

Three free checked bags when traveling within Alaska on Alaska Airlines.

Hawaii residents have their own community just for them: Huaka’i by Hawaiian. It offers benefits focused on interisland travel:

Don’t be persuaded by the names of the communities. Look a little deeper, because there’s a lot of overlap between destination lists. Compare the options against your upcoming travel plans.

You can change communities once per calendar year, so you aren’t locked in to your initial choice indefinitely. However, switching communities resets your destination-challenge progress, but you’ll retain any points you’ve already earned.

If your home airport (or one you visit frequently) is already on a community’s list, you may have an easier time earning points. Also consider whether a particular community benefit, such as free checked bags or points back on award flights for kids, outweighs the destination challenge.

Finally, pay attention to airport codes. For example, Tokyo’s Narita (NRT) and Haneda (HND) appear on different community lists. New York’s qualifying airport is JFK, while it is DCA in Washington, D.C. If you have a choice between airports, check which one qualifies for your community’s challenge.

#3166. What Security Teams Still Get Wrong About Asset Visibility

Yochai Corem is GM of Exposure Management at Check Point Software.

Organizations have more asset data than ever, but turning that data into a reliable view of what they own, operate and expose remains difficult.

A server appears in three tools with different identifiers. A cloud workload has an owner who has left the business. A forgotten website remains online after the campaign that funded it has ended. Each leaves security teams with information they still have to investigate before they can act.

Security leaders should judge asset visibility by how reliably it supports a decision. A useful inventory helps teams establish what an asset does, who is responsible for it, how it is protected and what should happen when a problem appears.

Several familiar assumptions prevent organizations from reaching that standard.

An incomplete configuration management database (CMDB) should prompt a review of how records are created, updated and retired.

Start by defining its purpose. The CMDB should record the systems, services and dependencies needed to manage business operations, with accountable owners and lifecycle status. Detailed security telemetry can remain in specialist systems, linked through reliable identifiers. An impersonating website belongs in an external threat register connected to the affected brand.

Then identify which sources can reveal missing records. Cloud platforms, endpoint management, identity providers, vulnerability scanners and procurement systems can each expose inconsistencies. Buying a service should trigger registration; retiring it should trigger checks that access, infrastructure and records have been removed.

Reconciliation should reflect the pace of change. I would set a daily reconciliation target for cloud and endpoint feeds; use deployment and retirement events to trigger updates; and review unresolved discrepancies weekly. Quarterly ownership reviews can supplement those checks.

An asset without an owner needs an interim accountable team and an escalation deadline. “Unknown” should describe an investigation in progress.

The modern attack surface spans internal infrastructure, cloud workloads, software as a service (SaaS), internet-facing systems, supplier connections and the organization’s broader digital presence.

Those categories need different forms of accountability. Infrastructure teams may manage servers, cloud teams manage workloads and business application owners sponsor SaaS services. Marketing or regional teams may control domains and social accounts. Supplier relationships require a business sponsor alongside technical oversight.

Security should establish a review and response process across those boundaries. A newly discovered domain needs ownership verification and an assessment of what it hosts. An unfamiliar SaaS service needs checks on its users, data and access. A fraudulent social account needs investigation and a disruption process.

Review frequency should follow exposure and change, with continuous discovery where practical and scheduled reviews for ownership, supplier access and dormant services. This broader scope aligns with the National Institute of Standards and Technology’s (NIST) inclusion of hardware, software, services and supplier services in asset management.

Leaders can test this assumption by following a newly discovered asset through the security process.

Does discovery trigger checks for required endpoint protection, vulnerability scanning and an accountable owner? Does an unmanaged internet-facing system reach someone authorized to restrict or remove it? Can the organization confirm that the resulting action occurred?

These outcomes show whether visibility operates as a security control. The Center for Internet Security’s (CIS) Control 1 connects inventory management with identifying and addressing unauthorized or unmanaged assets.

Useful measures include the time taken to assign ownership, the proportion of applicable assets covered by required controls and the age of unresolved discrepancies. A growing inventory count provides little reassurance when newly discovered systems remain unmanaged.

Additional tools introduce additional records, each with its own identifiers, timestamps and assumptions.

Effective reconciliation requires explicit rules. Stable cloud resource identifiers or device identifiers should take precedence over an Internet Protocol (IP) address that may change or be reused. Records need source and freshness information so teams can distinguish a current observation from a stale entry.

Organizations also need rules for conflicting fields. The identity system may establish who uses a device, while a service registry identifies the team accountable for the application it supports. Those relationships should remain distinguishable.

Cyber asset attack surface management (CAASM) can support this work. Organizations can also improve CMDB integrations, use cloud asset inventories and service catalogs, or build reconciliation workflows in their existing data platforms. The operational requirements remain consistent: reliable matching, clear authority for each field and a process for resolving exceptions.

Discovery should initiate a decision about management, protection or retirement.

A forgotten website needs an owner to confirm whether it still serves a business purpose. A vulnerable application needs assessment of its exposure, dependencies and operational constraints before a response is selected.

That decision should remain traceable through completion. If a service is retired, verify that it is no longer reachable. If a security control is applied, confirm that it covers the intended asset. Updating the inventory should accompany the operational change.

This ongoing connection between asset information and action also underpins continuous threat exposure management (CTEM).

CTEM depends on this discipline throughout scoping, discovery, prioritization, validation and mobilization.

An unknown asset cannot enter the prioritization process. A known asset without business context is difficult to rank. Missing dependencies weaken assessments of attack paths and change impact. An unknown owner delays action, while stale records undermine confirmation that an exposure has been reduced.

CAASM and external attack surface management (EASM) can support these stages by reconciling internal records and discovering internet-facing assets. Their value depends on the ownership, decision rules and response processes surrounding them.

Security leaders should expect asset visibility to connect discovery with accountable action. Every significant asset needs enough current context for someone to determine its protection requirements, address its exposures and verify the result. That is how a reliable inventory helps reduce the opportunities available to an attacker.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3165. BigBear.ai vs. Innodata: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

One is built on federal and defense contracts, the other on training AI models for big tech, and their financial profiles diverge sharply.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 5:29PM EST

10 stocks we like better than BigBear.ai ›

Choosing between these specialized AI plays requires balancing high-stakes government work against commercial data engineering growth. Investors must decide if BigBear.ai (BBAI -8.67%) or Innodata (INOD -8.91%) better suits their portfolio needs.

BigBear.ai provides complex decision intelligence tools for federal and defense agencies, while Innodata helps major tech firms train generative AI systems. Both companies operate in the rapidly evolving world of artificial intelligence but serve different markets. This comparison examines their financial health, customer concentration, and valuation to help you decide which stock fits your strategy.

BigBear.ai offers decision intelligence solutions that combine data from different sources to help leaders make faster choices. It serves critical sectors like national security, cybersecurity, and global supply chains. According to its latest annual report, a specific contract accounted for nearly 51% of consolidated revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $142.1 million. This represented a revenue growth decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior period. The company reported a net loss of roughly $180.2 million during this same time frame.

Following its early 2026 debt conversion, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.0x, meaning the company carries virtually no debt relative to its equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term debts with short-term assets, is close to 1.8x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was negative at nearly $46.3 million.

Innodata operates as a global data engineering firm that prepares and evaluates data for large-scale AI systems. The company serves several high-profile technology leaders, with one customer in its digital data solutions segment accounting for nearly 58% of total revenue in 2025. This focus on the best small cap tech stocks in the data sector highlights its role in the generative AI boom.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $350.2 million. This reflects a revenue growth increase of nearly 46.9% over the previous year. The company also achieved a net income of roughly $48.5 million during this period.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 2.7x. The debt-to-equity ratio is 0.0x, indicating a strong balance sheet with no total debt. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $35.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 23.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

BigBear.ai faces significant risk due to heavy revenue concentration with government customers who can terminate contracts for convenience. Scaling its sales efforts remains a challenge while managing long and unpredictable sales cycles. It also faces intense competition from Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) and Amazon (AMZN -4.10%). The company also navigates complex AI deployment risks and recent financial volatility including litigation over accounting errors.

Innodata relies heavily on a single customer that contributed nearly 58% of its 2025 revenues, making it vulnerable to contract changes. It competes against well-resourced firms like Accenture (ACN +1.19%) and Cognizant Technology Solutions (CTSH +3.11%) in the data engineering space. The business also deals with geopolitical risks from its global workforce and ongoing legal challenges regarding securities class actions.

Innodata appears to offer a more attractive value proposition based on its lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Innodata. AI models are only as good as the data they learn from, and Innodata helps big tech companies create and test that data. Its sales have grown year over year for 13 straight quarters, and it makes money.

Last quarter, sales jumped 53% and management raised its growth outlook, though the stock has fallen sharply this fall. There are risks: Two customers make up most of its sales, margins narrowed, and one program is winding down. But Innodata is adding new customers and work from a large cloud company, and expects to replace the lost program by early next year.

To give BigBear.ai its due, it does high-stakes work for national security and border security customers with contracts that are difficult to replace, and its backlog is growing. But it's still losing money, and government contract sales can be difficult to predict. That uncertainty is where Innodata stands apart: It is already profitable, with plenty of cash to keep investing as demand for AI data grows.

#3161. The Closer People Get To This AI Artwork, The More Nature Disappears

#3158. How To Teach Your AI Agent About Your Company

Frederik Riskær Pedersen, Founder and CEO of Maplith, a company brain for your AI agents.

For 16 years, I ran a translation company that grew from a handful of people into an international business. The most important thing I learned about knowledge is that it rarely lives where you think it does.

One customer shows it well. We had to fetch their files from their own system and deliver them in their own layout, and we could only invoice some of the repetitions. None of that was written down anywhere. It lived with one project manager. Anyone else would have done it the standard way and got it wrong.

That tailoring was not a side issue. It was the product. If the work had been standard, the customer could have run the model themselves. This is why a company’s rules never get standardized away: The exceptions are often exactly what customers pay for.

I think of this every time someone asks me why their AI agent isn’t delivering. The models are good enough. The problem is that the agent starts from zero every time. It does not know which systems you run, how they connect or which rules you follow without ever writing them down. So it guesses, and a confident guess in your accounts is worse than no answer at all.

Here are five things you can do this month before you let an agent work inside your business. None of them needs a new tool.

Book two hours with the three or four people who make most of the everyday decisions. Ask them one question: “What would a new hire ask you in their first week?” The answers are your unwritten rules: which customers get special terms, when a refund is approved, what can be invoiced and what cannot. Write each one as a short sentence, specific enough to check: “This customer gets files back in their own template.” “Refunds above 300 euros are approved by the finance lead.”

List every system you use and what each one is the source of truth for. You will find places where two systems describe the same thing differently. “Returned” in the ERP can mean the customer has sent the item back, while in the warehouse it means the item is back on the shelf.

The instinct is to make the systems agree. In my experience, that rarely works because each system was set up for the team that uses it, and that is why it works for them. Write the translation down instead. The same goes for numbers. In my company, the price did not sit in the same place from one customer to the next. Note where it lives for whom and which value to trust for which question.

Most useful answers need two systems joined: the order in the web shop and the invoice in accounting, or the support ticket and the customer in the CRM. Find the field that ties them together and test it by hand. Take 20 random orders and look for them in the other system. If you find 19, you can build on that link. If you find 12, you cannot, and an agent will rarely tell you it is guessing. The test takes an afternoon and saves months of wrong reports.

Split actions into four levels: read, propose, act after approval and act alone. Start with everything that changes something requiring approval from a named person. Set limits in money and volume, not in trust. When the agent has proposed the same action a hundred times without a mistake, move that one action up a level. That decision should be yours, never the agent’s.

For every action, record who asked for it, who approved it, what happened and when. It sounds bureaucratic, but it is the only way to answer when a customer, an auditor or your board asks, “Why did this happen?” Without a log, you also cannot tell when it is safe to give the agent more freedom.

None of this is about artificial intelligence. It is about making knowledge that lives in people’s heads, and in systems that do not talk to each other, available and trustworthy. Companies that do this get more out of their AI. They also become less fragile when a key person goes on holiday or leaves.

So start small. Book the two hours this week, write down 10 rules and test one link.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3157. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3152. Trust Is The New Battleground For Customer Engagement

James Garvert is the EVP of Fraud & Communications Solutions for TransUnion.

​Engaging with consumers has never been more essential, or more difficult. Amid the meteoric rise of AI-enabled scams, call spoofing and social engineering attacks, the challenges organizations face when trying to reach consumers seem insurmountable.

Every interaction depends on trust, yet consumers are more hesitant than ever to engage. Many of the traditional warning signs consumers were trained to recognize have disappeared—consumers are on edge.

And with good reason: It’s a jungle out there.

In our recent global study, “61% of business leaders ranked fraud among the risks with the greatest potential business impact, ahead of cybersecurity breaches, compliance, customer churn, operational cost and reputational risk. More concerning, 58% said they’re very or extremely concerned fraud is evolving faster than their organizations’ abilities to detect and prevent it.”

Historically, organizations have viewed fraud and engaging with customers as separate disciplines. But today’s threat environment makes it clear they’re deeply interconnected. In many cases, the communication channel itself—whether a phone call, text message, email or mobile app interaction—provides the first indication that fraud may be occurring.

Consumers don’t experience fraud, identity and communication challenges as separate issues. To them, it’s one experience, one problem. It’s a trust problem that’s reshaping the customer journey. In fact, 69% of consumers say they would switch vendors for stronger security.

Trust has become the foundation for both customer engagement and fraud prevention.

At our recent Fraud & Communications Summit in Chicago, keynote speaker Matt O’Neill, a former U.S. Secret Service Special Agent and recipient of Gold and Silver Awards for distinguished service, shared insights from years spent working undercover as a banker, broker, money launderer and other personas. In these roles, he led multinational investigations into crypto-laundering networks and dark web marketplaces.

According to O’Neill, fraudsters are winning because fraud has evolved into a highly organized industry. He explained that scam operations now function much like large multinational corporations. Scam compounds are similar to call centers, complete with organizational structures, performance quotas, shift schedules and human resources departments. Unfortunately, many of these compounds operate under sophisticated criminal networks and employ workers who have been trafficked.

At the same time, the barrier to entry into the fraud world has never been lower. AI enables fraudsters to create convincing scams at scale in virtually any language, including cloned voices, deepfake videos and personalized social engineering campaigns. And it’s cheap.​

The consequences of eroding trust extend well beyond fraud losses. Organizations often experience:

Ironically, the industries and brands most trusted by consumers are frequently among the most targeted by fraudsters, with the highest fraud instances related to auto lending, bank-issued and retail credit cards and unsecured personal lending.

According to Edelman, trust now rivals price and quality as a primary factor influencing consumer decision-making. Research shows that “77% of consumers cited confidence their personal data is secure as the most important feature when choosing whom to transact with online.”

Organizations can strengthen trust by approaching fraud prevention and customer communications as a unified strategy.

Traditional fraud controls were designed for predictable attacks. Today’s fraudsters adapt quickly, vary their tactics and coordinate attacks across multiple channels and sessions. Identity verification remains important, but it only confirms who someone claims to be. It doesn’t determine whether the current interaction should be trusted.

Here’s where identity verification falls short:

• KYC (Know Your Customer) controls are often strongest at onboarding, not throughout the customer lifecycle.

• Devices, behaviors and risk profiles change over time.

• Credentials can be compromised after verification.

• Synthetic identities can appear legitimate on paper.

The most important fraud signal is often not a single event. Any single signal can be manipulated or generate false positives. It’s the story that unfolds throughout an interaction. It emerges through relationships among users, devices, accounts, entities and sessions.

As if it wasn’t hard enough, AI has turbocharged fraud. In addition, labeling identified threats as AI-driven remains a challenge. That’s because it’s difficult to build comprehensive strategies with new AI fraud models evolving so rapidly.

While fraud practitioners know when an account was taken over, there’s currently no label for how it was taken over and whether AI drove the attack. As a result, the AI-attributed training set doesn’t exist yet for tracking that fraud, and detection stays anchored to heuristics and analyst judgment.

To be able to engage effectively with consumers and prevent fraud, organizations must adopt continuous authentication and layered risk strategies.

• Develop a comprehensive strategy—don’t address fraud in silos across your organization.

• Form strategic partnerships with your cyber teams.

• Correlate identity, device, behavioral and contextual signals in real time.

The process is straightforward: Collect. Correlate. Score. Decide.

Every phone call, text message, email and digital transaction represents a trust moment. Customers want confidence the organization contacting them is legitimate and their information and transactions are protected. Simply asking consumers to “trust us” is no longer enough.

Verified identity, authenticated communications and real-time risk intelligence are becoming the foundation of trust in a digital-first world.

The future isn’t reactive fraud mitigation. It’s proactive trust at the point of interaction, by orchestrating identity, risk and communications intelligence across every customer interaction.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3151. Here's the Average Social Security Benefit at Age 67

Full retirement age is the baseline for your check, but where most 67-year-olds actually land may surprise you.

By Kailey Hagen, CFP® – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:00PM EST

The average Social Security benefit for 67-year-olds is $2,016.48 per month, according to Social Security Administration (SSA) data from December 2025. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later. It's the point where you're eligible for your unreduced retirement amount.

Reaching this age doesn't always mean you receive exactly 100% of your baseline amount. Many people who are 67 today have a full retirement age of 66 years and 10 months. These workers may have already earned small delayed retirement credits if they waited until 67 to claim their checks.

Earnings histories also impact these averages. Men who are 67 receive an average of $2,234.41 per month, while the average among women is $1,801.82. This results in a monthly gender gap of $432.59 as of December 2025.

Think of your full retirement age as the baseline for your benefits. The SSA bases your check on your average monthly income over your 35 highest-earning years, adjusting those wages for inflation and applying the primary insurance amount formula. This calculation creates the standard amount you're entitled to at full retirement age.

You don't have to claim at your baseline. You can earn delayed retirement credits, which are increases for delaying past FRA, worth 8% per year up to age 70. For a worker whose FRA is 67, waiting those three extra years provides a 24% total increase in monthly income.

These decisions have a visible impact on the data. The average monthly benefit at age 70 is $2,274.68, which is $258.20 higher than the age-67 average.

But even for these claimants, benefits alone often aren't enough to live on. Social Security was only designed to replace about 40% of pre-retirement wages, so most retirees rely on savings or other income to cover their full costs.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027 to help benefits keep pace with inflation. This will add about $73 a month on top of the average listed above. These adjustments are designed to protect your purchasing power over time.

Knowing the population average gives you a target for your own planning, but your final check depends on your career earnings and when you choose to claim.

Waiting until age 67 to claim Social Security helps you avoid the early claiming benefit reduction, which can drastically reduce your checks. This is one major reason FRA is such a popular claiming age, but it's not the only one.

When you claim Social Security under your FRA, you're also at risk of losing some of your benefits to the earnings test if you're still working. In 2027, for example, you lose $1 from your checks for every $2 you earn over $25,960 if you're under your FRA all year. If you'll reach your FRA next year, you only lose $1 for every $3 you earn over $67,920.

These lost benefits come back to you as a permanent benefit boost at your FRA. But for some, the risk of short-term losses makes them delay their claim until their FRA, when the earnings test no longer applies. This is something to weigh if you plan to continue working once you're eligible for Social Security checks.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

#3149. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3148. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#3146. PayPal vs. SoFi Technologies: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

PayPal's global reach and SoFi's shift to profitability pull in opposite directions, and the valuation gap between them may tip the debate for 2027 investors.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 6:05PM EST

Deciding between PayPal (PYPL -5.46%) and SoFi Technologies (SOFI -7.76%) requires balancing established market dominance against aggressive digital growth. Both companies are fighting for a larger share of your digital wallet today.

PayPal remains a global giant in payment processing, while SoFi is a fast-growing digital bank targeting a younger, high-earning demographic. While they operate in different niches, both compete for the same pool of consumer financial activity, making them frequent candidates for a fintech-focused portfolio as investors look toward the future of money.

PayPal operates a massive digital payments platform serving nearly 439 million active accounts across 200 markets. It provides branded checkout services, person-to-person payments via Venmo, and even Bitcoin (CRYPTO:BTC) trading within its app. This scale makes it one of the most recognizable names in fintech stocks today as it adapts to a changing financial landscape.

Reliable recent quarterly figures for at least one of these companies aren't available, so this comparison uses each company's latest full fiscal year. For FY 2025, PayPal reported revenue of nearly $33.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 4.3% over the prior year. The company generated net income of close to $5.2 billion, showing a steady ability to convert its high volume of transactions into profit for its shareholders.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio reached roughly 0.5x, comparing total debt to shareholder equity. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to short-term liabilities, was approximately 1.3x. Free cash flow, or the cash remaining after paying for operations and equipment, was nearly $5.6 billion in FY 2025.

SoFi Technologies is a mobile-first financial services provider that offers a wide range of products including student loan refinancing, home loans, and investment tools. It serves approximately 15.8 million members and has expanded into business services through a partnership with Mastercard (MA -0.14%). The company also supports Bitcoin trading and provides modern banking infrastructure to other firms through its proprietary technology segments.

For its latest annual report covering FY 2025, SoFi Technologies generated revenue of nearly $3.6 billion, which was an increase of approximately 35.1% compared to the previous year. The company reported net income of close to $481.3 million, marking a significant transition into consistent profitability. This growth was driven by a diversified mix of lending products and financial services that attracted new members to its platform.

Based on its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was roughly 0.2x, helping investors see the low level of debt relative to shareholder equity. The current ratio was approximately 0.2x, which measures a company's ability to cover immediate liabilities with assets that can be quickly converted to cash. Free cash flow for the year was a net loss of nearly $4.0 billion, which often occurs in banking as capital is deployed into new loans rather than held as cash.

PayPal faces intense competition from both traditional banks and newer technology platforms that offer digital checkout solutions. The company is also subject to extensive global regulatory oversight regarding payment processing and anti-money laundering rules. Any failure to manage cybersecurity risks or protect user data could lead to significant penalties and damage its reputation.

SoFi Technologies must manage the complex regulatory requirements that come with being a bank holding company. It relies on a concentrated number of whole loan purchasers, meaning its results could suffer if these buyers reduce their activity. Additionally, the company faces the challenge of managing rapid growth across its various lending and technology segments without increasing credit risks.

When comparing these two companies using Forward P/E, PayPal looks significantly cheaper than its peer based on future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with SoFi Technologies. Its sales and earnings are both growing far faster than PayPal's, and it has built a full digital bank with more than 17 million members, starting from student loans. Its bank charter lets it fund loans with its members' deposits, a cost advantage most fintech rivals lack.

SoFi also gave investors its 2027 outlook a full quarter early, and it came in above analysts' expectations, with revenue expected to grow about 27%. Management built it assuming interest rates keep rising, even after a rate hike this year hurt the stock. The shares are still down for the year, so long-term investors get a lower price for a profitable, growing business.

To give PayPal its due, it is a household name that produces plenty of cash, and Venmo is growing fast. But its original business, letting shoppers pay online with their PayPal account, is barely growing, and overall sales are rising slowly. By contrast, SoFi's lending and banking businesses are surging, and even its technology business, which lost a large client, is expected to return to growth in 2027. For long-term investors, that mix of growth, profits, and a lower price makes SoFi the better buy for 2027.
