#3142. Prediction: Rocket Lab Will Triple Its Revenue by 2028. Here’s What That Would Mean for the Stock.

What sounds outlandish on the surface actually makes a lot of sense once you dive deeper into the details of this space-launch company’s efforts.

By James Brumley – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Rocket Lab ›

Plenty of companies are growing incredibly well right now, including several from the artificial intelligence business. A company that's set to triple its top line over the course of the next couple of years, though? That seems outrageous.

Except for orbital launch and satellite tech specialist Rocket Lab (RKLB -10.92%), it isn't. This future business is already lined up. These paying customers are just waiting for one pivotal milestone that's likely to materialize sometime next year. Then the floodgates open.

Rocket Lab offers satellite launch solutions. Its relatively small Electron rocket design has now been launched 100 times, putting 273 satellites into low earth orbit.

The organization also provides a range of satellite components and construction services, which accounts for more than half of last quarter's revenue of $268 million. Rocket Lab expects to do around $980 billion in business in fiscal 2026, well up from last year's $602 billion.

This is just a taste of what's to come, though. As the company announced with its third-quarter report posted earlier this month, Rocket Lab's backlog grew 52% from Q2's $2.36 billion to an incredible $3.6 billion. The organization just needs more capacity, but that's coming. Rocket Lab can now manufacture an Electron rocket every eight days.

Then there's the proverbial 800-pound gorilla in the room. That's the company's larger Neutron rocket, which has seen its inaugural flight pushed back several times, with the first flight now expected sometime in 2027.

Despite the delays so far, once Neutron is flying, everything takes a turn for the better. As CFO Adam Spice commented during the third-quarter's earnings conference call, adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) "will turn positive within six months of that first successful test flight," with cash flow likely to turn positive shortly after that.

Neutron isn't the big profit center in and of itself, to be clear. It's just a means to an end. Being able to put larger, heavier satellites into orbit also opens up its satellite tech business. Of its new backlog of $3.6 billion, $1.7 billion of it reflects space systems rather than launch services, up 194% year over year.

To this end, while analysts aren't yet looking for Rocket Lab's revenue to triple between now and 2028, with recent failures of similar rockets like ULA's Vulcan and one earlier in the year from Blue Origin, Rocket Lab is quickly becoming a preferred launch option. It may well exceed 2028's expectations.

As for the impact on the stock, it's not a complicated prediction to make. If the company's top line comes anywhere close to tripling by 2028, the bullish argument firms up enormously.

While it may not triple in value in step with revenue, $3 billion in sales could turn this high-growth $34 billion outfit into a $60 billion outfit with relative ease. And given that the company isn't likely to raise money by issuing more stock anytime soon, that translates into a near-doubling of the stock's price.

You're getting some help in the meantime, by the way. The analyst community overwhelmingly considers RKLB stock a strong buy right now, with a 12-month consensus price target of $93.30 already more than 80% above the ticker's current price.

#3138. Prediction: Nvidia Stock Will Do This in the 6 Months After a New CEO Takes Over, According to History

Nvidia is searching for a new CEO following Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:42PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) is navigating one of the most consequential leadership transitions in recent Silicon Valley history. Following founder Jensen Huang's unexpected resignation amid a controversy involving alleged connections to Chinese intelligence, investors are growing increasingly concerned about the company's future. This uncertainty is weighing heavily on Nvidia stock, even as its underlying business continues to deliver extraordinary financial results.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's former chief financial officer, is now serving as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent successor. According to reports, the company is considering external candidates, including Arm Holdings CEO Rene Haas.

History offers some clues about how technology stocks behave following major CEO transitions. However, Nvidia's circumstances are quite different from those of its peers, making the company's financial outlook particularly important right now.

Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) represents a successful example of a technology leadership transition. Satya Nadella became CEO on Feb. 4, 2014, replacing Steve Ballmer after Microsoft conducted a formal succession search. Nadella had previously led Microsoft's cloud and enterprise businesses, giving him extensive knowledge of the company's operations.

Microsoft stock advanced roughly 19% during Nadella's first six months as CEO. Of course, over the long term, he helped transform the Windows developer into a cloud computing powerhouse.

Alphabet (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%) offers another example. Sundar Pichai became CEO of Alphabet on Dec. 3, 2019, succeeding co-founder Larry Page. However, Pichai had already served as Google's CEO since 2015, making the transition more of a consolidation of leadership responsibilities than an unexpected management overhaul.

Alphabet stock was higher roughly six months later, despite experiencing a meaningful drawdown during the short-lived COVID-19 market crash. Moreover, Page and his co-founder Sergey Brin remained involved as shareholders and directors, providing continuity that Nvidia currently lacks.

Amazon (AMZN -4.10%) experienced a different outcome when Andy Jassy succeeded Jeff Bezos on July 5, 2021. Jassy was another internal executive who had previously built and led Amazon Web Services (AWS). Although Bezos relinquished the CEO position, he remains executive chairman, preserving his influence over the company's long-term strategy.

Amazon shares declined approximately 6% during Jassy's first six months as CEO. At the time, investors were assessing the impacts of slowing pandemic-era growth and rising operating expenses on Amazon's e-commerce marketplace.

The three examples above show that CEO succession can bring different short-term outcomes. However, they also share an important characteristic: Each of these companies promoted an experienced internal executive through an orderly transition, unlike Nvidia's abrupt leadership disruption.

Nvidia's situation is unusual because Huang wasn't just another corporate executive. He co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and spent more than three decades directing the company's evolution from a graphics chip manufacturer into the world's leading supplier of artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure. His influence extended across engineering, product development, corporate culture, and customer relationships.

As a result of his departure, investors are increasingly questioning whether Nvidia can maintain its technological edge without the executive who helped establish it. The uncertainty is compounded by growing competition from custom AI accelerators developed by Alphabet, Amazon, and other hyperscalers. Nvidia's next CEO needs to prove they can preserve the company's innovation-driven culture while navigating complicated supply chains and regulatory relationships.

Despite the unusual circumstances, Nvidia's financial outlook provides a compelling reason to anticipate a meaningful recovery. During its fiscal third-quarter earnings call, management increased its fiscal 2028 revenue growth forecast to 75%, up from its previous outlook of 70%. This suggests demand for Nvidia's accelerated computing platforms remains strong despite Huang's departure.

Furthermore, Nvidia expects to generate $130 billion in revenue during the fourth quarter -- representing 91% growth year over year. The company is also forecasting gross margins of between 72% and 73% in fiscal 2028, suggesting that accelerating sales growth will translate into additional profits.

Nvidia's share repurchase program provides another subtle catalyst for the stock. In September, the board increased the company's existing authorization by $150 billion, leaving approximately $227.3 billion available at the end of the third quarter. Management intends to execute the remaining authorization by the end of fiscal 2028.

By reducing the number of outstanding shares, stock buybacks can increase earnings per share (EPS) even if net income is unchanged. Combined with Nvidia's revenue growth and profit margins, upcoming share repurchases should fuel substantial EPS expansion over the coming year.

My prediction is that Nvidia stock will trade higher six months after its new CEO assumes the role. The appointment itself should remove the primary source of uncertainty around the company, allowing investors to concentrate once again on revenue growth, earnings power, and competitive advantages.

#3137. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#3135. When To End A Technology Pilot: 19 Warning Signs

Technology pilots give organizations an opportunity to test new solutions on a limited scale, evaluate their potential and identify challenges before committing to a broader rollout. But not every pilot warrants further investment, and knowing when to end one can be just as important as knowing when to expand it.

For tech leaders, the challenge is distinguishing between setbacks that can be addressed by adjusting a pilot’s scope or timeline and more fundamental issues that call its viability into question. Below, members of Forbes Technology Council share warning signs that indicate it’s time to end a technology pilot rather than extend it, redesign it or move it into production.

A pilot without success criteria is not a pilot. Defining boundaries and expectations, and, importantly, being transparent, are critical to a pilot’s success. Users should also share any uncertainty during the pilot instead of waiting for it to pivot or improve. - Arunava Sen, Tata Communications America Inc.

A clear sign is when the pilot team cannot tell you what the technology accessed, what decisions it made, or what data it touched. If you cannot answer those questions in a controlled environment (test group), you will not answer them in production. - Michael Nicosia, Salt Security

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

One sign a technology pilot shouldn’t progress doesn’t involve the new tech itself but the tools teams already work with. If teams are working in siloed workflows with tools that don’t collaborate with one another, introducing new technology can drive up costs, increase complexity and hurt productivity. Connected tools and workflows are integral to the success of any new tech deployment. - Usman Shuja, Bluebeam

End a pilot when its success depends on hand-curated data that won’t exist in production. If someone cleaned the dataset, resolved definitions and fixed edge cases for the pilot, and no one owns doing that at scale in production, extending or redesigning the product in production won’t help. In many cases, technology isn’t the problem; the foundation is. Ask not whether the pilot worked, but what it worked on. - Prakash Reddy, Okta

If the pilot only works while its champion is in the room or while the vendor quietly does manual work behind the scenes, you’ve tested enthusiasm, not technology. Hand it to a team that didn’t build it for 30 days. If results collapse, you’d be pushing a dependency into production, not a tool. End it and keep the learnings. - Damian Wasserman, BEON.tech

When the team stops asking what we learned and starts asking when we get to stop, that’s the sign. A pilot without defined success criteria is avoidance. If you can’t name the specific business result it was supposed to prove or disprove, extending it won’t help. End it, define the outcome you actually need, and redesign around that from the start. - Ali Alkhafaji, APPLY

Organizations should end a technology pilot when they have full visibility of the tool’s usage and cost metrics but still cannot connect either to a meaningful business outcome. Adoption alone is not enough without a clear path to measurable value, and extending the pilot risks turning experimentation into ongoing spend without a business case. - Conal Gallagher, Flexera

A clear sign is when the people who were supposed to benefit stop asking about it. Pilots drag on because the sponsors like the idea, not because users need it. If the pilot group has quietly gone back to their old way of working and nobody complains when the tool goes down, there’s nothing to extend. Name the lesson, thank the team and end it. - Hastimal Jangid, Coozmoo Digital Solutions, Inc.

A pilot should be reconsidered when it performs well in a demo but can’t prove that it solves a real business problem. AI makes it easy to add capabilities, but more features don’t always mean more value. Teams need clear measures of success and feedback loops to understand what’s working and what’s not before investing further. - Tony Grout, M-Files

A clear sign to end a technology pilot is when it repeatedly fails to demonstrate measurable business value despite validated assumptions and reasonable adjustments. If the solution cannot deliver meaningful ROI, operational efficiency or strategic impact, extending it only increases sunk costs. Strong leadership means knowing when to stop, not just when to scale. - Kameshwar Singh, Cohesity

End a pilot if no effort was spent defining the vision and how value will be realized at scale. That’s like building a bridge from both ends without deciding where it should meet. - Venky Ramesh, LatentView Analytics

A pilot should end when success depends on conditions that production will never have: handpicked data, expert users, manual fixes, privileged integrations or constant vendor support. If removing that scaffolding collapses the value case, extending the pilot only perfects the illusion. The real test is not, “Does it work?” but, “Does it still create measurable value when exposed to ordinary users, messy data, real constraints and an ordinary day?” - Nicola Sfondrini, PWC

End a pilot when making the technology operable, reliable and governable outweighs its business value. If protecting sensitive data, maintaining an audit trail and providing human oversight would cost more than the expected benefit, extending that particular pilot needs a stronger business case than the time and money already invested. - Brian Stimpfl, S-Docs

End a pilot when the evidence invalidates its core business premise. If users can complete the workflow but gain no meaningful time, cost or quality improvement over the current process, more engineering won’t create demand. Define that minimum benefit before launch, test it with real users, and stop when it remains out of reach. - Irina Shymko, Langate Software

End the pilot when the team cannot define a credible path from the experiment to a measurable business outcome. If success still depends on vague future improvements, heroic manual support or assumptions the pilot was supposed to validate, extending it usually just delays the decision. - Dhyey Mavani, Abridge

A pilot should end when the team cannot explain what new evidence another extension will produce. Every extra week should answer a specific open question about value, risk, adoption, cost or scale. Once the remaining time is only keeping the pilot alive, the experiment has already delivered its answer. - Sibasis Padhi, Walmart Inc.

I would end a pilot when it successfully completes individual tasks but repeatedly fails to deliver the full business outcome. Often, the underlying issue is the absence of a clear decision model for handling exceptions, approvals or unresolved work across teams. If nobody can authorize what happens next, scaling the technology only scales the problem. - Vamshi Noothi, Cisco

A pilot should be ended if nobody uses it unless someone reminds them. A pilot that only stays alive while its sponsor keeps pushing won’t survive production, and extending it just postpones that answer. Software nobody uses isn’t free; it still costs money to run, maintain and support. End it, write down what you learned about the problem, and put the team on something people actually want. - Jeremy Suriel, Kustomer

A clear sign to end a technology pilot is when no business owner is willing to take responsibility for implementation. Even if the technology performs well, success requires business ownership, accountability and commitment to change. Without a sponsor willing to champion the solution, allocate resources and own the outcomes, extending the pilot is unlikely to deliver value. Technology cannot create lasting benefits without clear business ownership. - Eranda Maldeniya, Enterprise Analytics

#3134. Here's How Much Nvidia Stock Is Worth Without Jensen Huang, According to Dan Ives

Dan Ives revised his price target on Nvidia stock following Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:10PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) has experienced a stunning reversal following founder Jensen Huang's unexpected resignation amid controversy surrounding alleged connections to Chinese intelligence. Investors are increasingly questioning whether the semiconductor behemoth can maintain its technological edge without the executive who spent more than three decades building the company. Those concerns have contributed to a dramatic sell-off, even as Nvidia continues reporting ridiculously good financial results.

The magnitude of the decline is anything but trivial. Nvidia stock reached a record closing price of around $239 in early October but has fallen to roughly $136 as of Nov. 20. That represents a decline of roughly 43%, wiping out more than $2.5 trillion in market capitalization and pushing Nvidia from the world's most valuable public company to fourth place.

Nevertheless, Wall Street analyst Dan Ives thinks investors are overlooking Nvidia's underlying earnings power. Although he acknowledges that Huang's departure changes the company's investment thesis, his revised price target suggests meaningful upside remains.

Ives has been particularly vocal about the consequences of Huang's resignation. During a recent interview, he estimated that Huang contributed $1 trillion to Nvidia's valuation. In a separate appearance, Ives described Huang as the company's "heart and lungs," emphasizing his influence over Nvidia's technological roadmap and competitive position.

Ives says Nvidia stock is worth $225 without Huang and $300 with him as CEO. These figures provide an interesting way to quantify the so-called Jensen premium. The difference between Ives' two valuations is $75 per share, representing a 25% reduction from his original $300 target.

The implied difference in market capitalization is actually considerably larger than the $1 trillion Ives alluded to. With approximately 24.2 billion shares outstanding, a $75 difference translates into roughly $1.8 trillion in equity value. At Ives' respective price targets, Nvidia would command a market capitalization of approximately $7.3 trillion with Huang and $5.4 trillion without him.

The difference in valuation illustrates the enormous importance Wall Street places on Nvidia's founder and why investors have reacted so negatively to his departure.

Huang's value extends beyond his responsibilities as CEO. Since co-founding Nvidia in 1993, he has overseen the company's transformation from a graphics processing company into the dominant supplier of accelerated computing infrastructure. His investments in GPUs, CUDA software, and AI computing helped position Nvidia at the center of the technology industry's most important transformation since the birth of the internet.

Perhaps Huang's greatest contribution has been leading Nvidia's culture of innovation. Huang consistently anticipated computing trends years before they became commercially relevant. This enabled Nvidia to develop new chip architectures and software ecosystems that competitors have struggled to replicate. This foresight helps establish durable competitive advantages and contributes to the company's pricing power.

Huang also cultivated relationships with other technology executives, hyperscalers, governments, and AI labs. These supply chain connections have become important as Nvidia participates in data center build-outs that require complex financing arrangements and long-term strategic commitments.

With all of that said, Nvidia's competitive advantages are not going to disappear just because Huang stepped away. The company's hardware and software ecosystem, engineering workforce, customer relationships, and product roadmap remain intact.

Moreover, Ives acknowledges that Nvidia has a lead of two to three years over its competitors in AI chip technology. Although Huang's departure could narrow that advantage over time, the company's existing leadership provides a robust foundation for future growth.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Despite reducing his Nvidia price target from $300 to $225, Ives maintains a bullish outlook on the company. At approximately $136 per share, his revised target implies roughly 65% upside, while his original $300 estimate implies 121% potential appreciation.

Importantly, Ives' optimism centers on the insatiable demand for AI computing infrastructure and Nvidia's technological advantages. Following the company's earnings report on Nov. 17, Ives wrote that the results answered a critical question: "Demand did not leave with him."

The financial evidence supports that assessment. Nvidia generated $115.8 billion in fiscal third-quarter revenue, representing 103% growth year over year, while data center revenue surged 110% to $107.6 billion. The company also reported a gross margin of 74.8%, demonstrating that its pricing power remains strong.

Furthermore, Nvidia forecast $130 billion in revenue for the fourth quarter and raised its fiscal 2028 revenue growth outlook to 75%. This forecast suggests the company is poised for further expansion despite the leadership transition.

The central takeaway from Ives' analysis is that Nvidia doesn't necessarily need Huang to remain an attractive investment. Although his departure has reduced -- for now -- the premium investors are willing to pay, his price target still suggests considerable appreciation over the coming year. For investors who can stomach short-term volatility, Nvidia's recent sell-off could represent an opportunity to buy the dip in the most dominant AI infrastructure business at a significantly discounted valuation.

#3131. Bristol Myers Squibb's Growth Portfolio Just Hit $7.7 Billion. Is This Cheap Pharma Stock a No-Brainer Buy?

The good news is that Bristol Myers Squibb's new drugs are growing; the bad news is that one old drug is nearing its patent expiration.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Bristol Myers Squibb ›

Bristol Myers Squibb (BMY +0.79%) is a large, well-respected pharmaceutical company. It traces its history back to the mid-1800s. Right now, the company's portfolio of newer drugs, which it calls its "growth portfolio", just generated nearly $7.7 billion in revenue in a single quarter. That's a 12% increase over the same quarter in 2025. But is the stock worth buying? The risk/reward balance looks tilted toward reward for all but the most conservative investors. Here's why.

The success Bristol Myers Squibb is having with its growth portfolio clearly indicates that this pharmaceutical giant hasn't lost its mojo. But investors are still downbeat on the stock (more on this below). So the dividend yield is a lofty 4.3%. The S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, the average healthcare stock yields 1.3%, and the average drug stock yields 1.3%. That above-market and industry yield will likely be very enticing to long-term dividend investors. Meanwhile, the roughly 55% dividend payout ratio leaves ample room for adversity and should mollify all but the most conservative income lovers.

Notably, the dividend has been increased regularly, though not annually, for over three decades. There have been no dividend cuts along the way despite the company working through some difficult periods during that span. That shows the board of directors is strongly committed to returning value to shareholders through a reliable, slowly growing dividend. Adding to the allure is the fact that the stock's 13x price-to-earnings ratio is notably below the pharma industry average of roughly 28x. The high yield and relatively low P/E ratio will likely be of interest to value investors.

Growth investors will appreciate the above-highlighted success of the company's growth portfolio. That portfolio is a collection of over a dozen new drugs that, as of the third quarter, account for nearly 60% of the company's drug revenues. That's good news and bad news, however, and it highlights the risk that investors face if they decide to buy Bristol Myers Squibb.

The healthcare giant's legacy portfolio still accounts for around 40% of revenues, or roughly $5.4 billion. And one drug, Eliquis, is responsible for nearly $4.7 billion of that total. Eliquis is set to see generic competition as soon as 2028. When that happens, the revenue this drug produces will likely start to fall, and perhaps sharply. That is why investors remain downbeat on Bristol Myers Squibb's stock despite the success of its growth portfolio.

It is reasonable that extremely conservative investors might want to stay on the sidelines until after Eliquis' patent expires. However, it is important to remember that patent expirations are just a normal part of the pharmaceutical business. And given the success of the growth portfolio, Bristol Myers Squibb continues to execute well in new drug development. There's just a mismatch between when patents expire, which happens on a set schedule, and its development efforts, which do not happen on a set schedule.

In order to buy Bristol Myers Squibb, you need to believe it will get through this period. History suggests it will, and that's backed up by the success of the growth portfolio.

If you can't handle the uncertainty around the patent expiration of Eliquis, you shouldn't buy Bristol Myers Squibb. However, for most investors, including dividend, value, and perhaps even growth-focused types, the stock is probably worth a deep dive. The market doesn't appear to be giving the company enough credit for its success on the development front, and that is probably tilting the risk-reward scale toward reward.

#3128. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#3126. The S&P 500 Has More Than Tripled in 10 Years, Even After Its Latest Pullback. Here’s What $1,000 in VOO Could Have Become.

A host of concerns are creating the perfect storm right now, harming market sentiment.

By Neil Patel – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:23PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

From the start of 2026 to its peak on Oct. 6, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%), the measuring stick for how the U.S. equity market is performing, posted a price return of 14%. Since that all-time high, though, the benchmark has fallen about 10% (as of Nov. 19).

Investors might be worried about a few things. They could be more skeptical about all the artificial intelligence (AI) data center spending that's taking place. Elevated inflation and rising interest rates are stealing the show from a macro perspective. And we're past a midterm election cycle that saw the Democrats take control of Congress, introducing regulatory uncertainty.

Step back. Even after its recent pullback, the S&P 500 index has risen 229% in 10 years. Here's what a $1,000 investment made in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -3.42%) a decade ago could have become today.

The S&P 500 index climbed at a 12.6% annualized rate over the past decade, before dividends. Including dividends, a $1,000 investment in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF 10 years ago would be worth about $3,953 today. That's a 14.7% annualized total return. Investors almost quadrupled their money.

The recent drop is a blip on the big-picture radar. Even after sliding since early October, the index is still up slightly in 2026.

What powered the gains? It's mostly earnings. In December 2016, the S&P 500 traded at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 17. This compares the index's price to the profits analysts expect over the next 12 months. Today, the multiple is around 18, slightly below its 10-year average.

Based on this metric, the valuation barely moved. Forward earnings per share, meanwhile, jumped from $133 (at the end of 2016) to $416 in early November of this year, a 212% increase. Investors didn't make money by paying higher prices. They built wealth because American businesses became far more profitable.

One sector stands above the rest. Information technology, measured by the Vanguard Information Technology ETF (VGT -7.00%) delivered an annualized total return of roughly 22.7% in the last decade. AI, cloud computing, and surrounding internet-based secular trends made trillion-dollar market caps a common theme. The tech sector now represents 37% of the S&P 500 index. VOO investors own those winners automatically, for an expense ratio of just 0.03%.

Those gains weren't easy to hold on to. The S&P 500 index fell nearly 20% in late 2018, crashed 34% during the COVID-19 panic, slid 25% in the 2022 bear market, and dropped 19% during the 2025 tariff scare. This year alone has brought two declines of 8% or more. Volatility is just a part of the game.

Each time the market fell, selling felt like the right move. But each time, the index eventually bounced back and went on to new highs.

Investors who trade in and out usually pay for it. Morningstar's (MORN -0.30%) latest Mind the Gap study found that the average dollar in U.S. mutual funds and ETFs earned 8.7% per year over the decade ended 2025. The funds themselves returned 9.9%. The difference is due to poorly timed buying and selling.

Missing a few days hurts, too. According to J.P. Morgan (JPM -3.03%) Asset Management, sitting out the 10 best days over the 20-year period from 2006 through 2025 would have cut an investor's annualized return from 11% to 6.6%, an enormous difference.

Dollar-cost averaging is the solution. This means investing a set amount on a regular schedule, regardless of price. There's no guessing involved. Adding steadily in this manner to the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF is no doubt one of the simplest ways to build wealth.

When the market is down, like it is now, hang in there.The best results of the past decade came from holding through every pullback.

#3122. Report: Goldman Sachs Predicts the S&P 500 Will Gain 26% in 12 Months

Despite a rocky few weeks on Wall Street, the next 12 months are looking much more bullish, according to Goldman Sachs.

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The stock market has had a rough few weeks. As of Nov. 20, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) had declined by about 10.4% in the prior month, largely driven by investor worries over the sustainability of the artificial intelligence (AI) trade. The tech-heavy Nasdaq-100 index has declined almost enough to qualify as a bear market, with a drop of nearly 20% since its Oct. 6 record high.

But according to Bloomberg reporting from Nov. 13, Goldman Sachs (GS -6.71%) strategists are bullish on the S&P 500 for next year. Recent research from Goldman suggests that the benchmark S&P index will gain 26% in the next 12 months by November 2027, despite possibly lower earnings growth compared to 2026.Let's look at what this might mean for investors -- and why it's a good sign to keep buying stocks with low-cost index funds like S&P 500 ETFs.

Goldman strategists predict that the S&P 500 will reach 8,700 points in the next 12 months. On Nov. 20, the index closed at about 6,910, so this would imply a gain of about 26% from that level.

That's not an outlandish return to expect for the S&P 500. A 26% gain in 12 months would be an exceptionally strong year, much better than the average. But it's well within the realm of possibility.

Keep in mind that the long-term average annual return for the S&P 500, based on historical data since 1928, is 9.98%. And in the past few years, the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -3.42%) has delivered annual returns of about 17.8% in 2025, 25% in 2024, and 26.3% in 2023.

Why does Goldman think the S&P 500 will do so well in the next 12 months? If so many investors are nervous about tech companies spending too much on AI data centers, isn't now a bad time to buy the S&P? After all, as of Nov. 19, eight of the top 10 holdings in the S&P 500 were tech names, and those top eight stocks made up about 33.5% of the index by weight.

Recent investor skepticism about AI stocks might actually be healthy for future returns. Recent declines in tech stock prices have driven down those companies' valuations. Goldman's research finds that AI hyperscalers are trading "well below" their historic valuations and says that "we see Technology -- and Hyperscalers in particular -- as relatively defensive."

The very same tech stocks that have been going through a correction in the past month might be poised for bigger gains in the next 12 months. Goldman says that even though some of the biggest hyperscalers have negative cash flow due to their aggressive AI investments, these companies still have strong balance sheets and could become attractive in the next 12 months if growth opportunities elsewhere are harder to find.

This trend of lower valuations can also be seen if you look at the S&P 500 index forward price-to-earnings ratio (P/E). This metric shows the index's earnings multiple based on the next 12 months' estimated earnings. The S&P forward P/E has dropped from a recent high of 30 to 21.08, trading at about a 15% discount compared to its five-year average.

S&P 500 P/E Ratio Forward Estimate data by YCharts

Long-term investors need to think beyond today, tomorrow, or even the next 12 months. One year is not "long term." But this Goldman Sachs research is a helpful sign that there are many reasons to be optimistic about the stock market, despite some recent volatility.

If you share Goldman's optimism about the S&P 500 in 2027, now can be a good time to buy low-cost index funds like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO). This ultra-low-cost ETF lets you track the performance of the S&P.

You don't have to pick stocks or worry about which companies or industries are winning or losing; this Vanguard ETF lets you own 500 of the largest publicly traded companies in America, representing about 80% of the total U.S. stock market. If Goldman's analysis is on target, your investment in the ETF could go up by 26% in the next 12 months.

But even if that forecast doesn’t come true, you can rest assured that the S&P 500 has delivered strong average annual returns of about 10% for long-term investors who can buy and hold through temporary ups and downs along the way.

#3119. Here's Why Nvidia Stock Crashed on Friday

Reports of a new investigation drove many investors to sell.

By Joe Tenebruso – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:25PM EST

Shares of Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) plunged 14% on Friday following reports that the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) is investigating the tech titan's accounting practices.

It's a challenging time to be an Nvidia shareholder. The dominant designer of artificial intelligence (AI) chips has shed more than a quarter of its value since Jensen Huang stepped down as CEO on Nov. 12.

On Thursday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta served an investigative subpoena on Nvidia related to Huang's reported affair with an alleged Chinese spy. "My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang's actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information -- and if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law," Bonta said.

And on Friday, Reuters reported that the SEC is probing Nvidia's accounting practices. Reuters noted that "Investigations are not evidence of wrongdoing and many close without resulting in enforcement action." Still, the report adds to the fear and uncertainty surrounding the company following Huang's departure.

Nvidia's share price is now down nearly 50% from its 52-week high of $243.37 in October. It has lost a staggering $2.8 trillion in market capitalization during that time.

Nvidia's quarterly results released on Nov. 17 were impressive. Revenue rose 103% year over year to $115.8 billion. Net income increased 105% to $65.3 billion.

However, Nvidia's regulatory and legal issues are driving more investors to question whether its torrid growth will continue. And buyers are demanding a lower price to accept those risks.

#3113. Chipotle Mexican Grill vs. Hershey: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Hershey trades at a lower forward P/E than Chipotle, but the gap raises a bigger question about which stock is the better buy.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 8:45PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Chipotle Mexican Grill ›

Choosing between Chipotle Mexican Grill (CMG -2.07%) and Hershey (HSY +0.82%) depends on whether you value aggressive expansion or reliable brand power.

Chipotle leads the fast-casual space with its focus on fresh ingredients and digital efficiency. Hershey dominates the confectionery market with a massive portfolio of snacks and sweets. This comparison helps you decide which of these consumer giants better aligns with your financial goals heading into 2027.

Chipotle Mexican Grill focuses on its mission of serving real ingredients across more than 4,000 restaurants in the United States, Canada, Europe, and the Middle East. The company relies on a robust digital ordering platform that accounts for over 16% of food and beverage revenue, utilizing third-party delivery aggregators to reach more customers. It also expands its global presence through key partnerships, such as the one with Alshaya Group for international restaurant operations.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $12.7 billion, representing growth of approximately 7.9% compared to the prior period. The company reported net income of close to $1.4 billion, which reflects a net margin of approximately 10.8% for the period.

Hershey operates as a dominant force among consumer discretionary stocks through its massive portfolio of chocolate, sweets, and mints. The company sells its products through wholesale distributors and mass merchandisers, with McLane Company accounting for approximately 27% of consolidated net sales in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business since the loss of such a large partner could significantly impact overall sales.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 27, 2026, revenue reached close to $12.3 billion, an increase of roughly 6.7% over the previous 12 months. The company achieved net income of approximately $1.8 billion, which contributed to a net margin of nearly 14.6% during this timeframe.

Chipotle Mexican Grill faces significant risks related to food safety and potential food-borne illness incidents, which can severely damage its brand reputation and increase operating costs. The company also deals with intense competition from traditional restaurants and newer entities like ghost kitchens or delivery services such as Starbucks (SBUX -1.28%). Furthermore, the business must navigate legal and regulatory challenges regarding labor practices.

Hershey is sensitive to the availability and pricing volatility of key raw materials such as cocoa, sugar, and dairy. Supply chain disruptions caused by climate change or natural disasters could negatively impact its manufacturing operations across its global facilities. The company also faces pressure to successfully implement digital transformation and strategic business realignment programs to stay competitive against other snack manufacturers in a rapidly evolving retail environment.

Hershey appears cheaper than Chipotle Mexican Grill based on traditional metrics.

Chipotle is a story about growth and Hershey is about rebounding profitability, and knowing that can help find what the best fit for a portfolio is. For Chipotle, revenue increased 9.6% in the third quarter of 2026 to $3.3 billion. Transactions increased by 0.8%, comparable sales rose 2.1%, and digital sales jumped 38.8%. On top of that, Chipotle opened 84 new locations and expects to open up to 370 new restaurants by the end of 2026. That said, its operating margin dropped from 15.9% to 13.7%, and earnings per share dipped 6.9% despite the noticeable increase in revenue growth. Overall, while revenue growth and location expansion look impressive, shareholders will want to make sure higher costs aren't cutting too deeply into profitability.

For Hershey in its third quarter, while revenue increased only 3%, adjusted earnings per share (EPS) rose 103% and net income climbed 112%. Gross margin also increased from 32.6% to 46.1%. That said, organic sales increased only 0.8%, while international sales fell 7.5%. What Hershey is seeing appears to be improved results driven by better margins and lower costs, rather than pure consumer demand.

Between the two, if you prioritize more long-term sales growth potential, Chipotle may be the better fit for a portfolio. For anyone looking for a more mature business model with improving profitability, Hershey may be the better option.

#3110. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#3108. Amazon's re:Invent Conference Starts Nov. 30. Here's What the Stock Did in the Month After the Last 10.

The cloud giant's biggest week of the year is nearly here. Has the stock usually climbed after it?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:52PM EST

Amazon (AMZN -4.10%) has a big week ahead. Its cloud computing arm, Amazon Web Services (AWS), hosts its yearly re:Invent conference in Las Vegas from Monday, Nov. 30, to Friday, Dec. 4. AWS calls re:Invent its flagship annual conference, and it's where the unit launches many new products every year.

It's tempting to think the stock might get a boost when AWS shows off what it's built. To find out, I checked how Amazon shares did in the month after each of the past 10 conferences, from 2016 through 2025, and compared that with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%).

Shares trade near $257 as I write, up about 12% this year and well ahead of the S&P 500's rise of about 5%. Amazon now stands about 9% below its Aug. 3 record close of $284.02.

Not really. For every year, I measured from the close on the trading day before the conference started to the close a month after opening day, using the same window for the S&P 500.

Amazon stock rose in just four of the 10 windows. It beat the index in four of them, too. And its median return was a drop of around 0.7%, but the S&P 500's median was a gain of about 2.1%.

The best year was 2024, when Amazon climbed around 6% in the month after the conference while the index dropped about 3%.

The worst was 2022. Other factors can easily outweigh a week of product news, and that year the stock had already lost about 44% before the conference began. It slid another 12% in the month that followed, compared with a 6% decline in the S&P 500.

Last year was closer to a wash. After the event ran from Dec. 1 to Dec. 5, 2025, Amazon stock slipped about 1% for the month, while the index was about flat.

In short, there's no consistent post-conference rally in the record. Amazon has lagged the S&P 500 more often than not after re:Invent -- in six of those 10 windows.

The week of the conference hasn't been much better. Measured from the previous close to that Friday's close, Amazon stock fell during six of those 10 conference weeks.

The 2024 event makes the strongest case that AWS's news can matter. That year, AWS rolled out its Nova family of artificial intelligence (AI) models and made servers built with its Trainium2 AI chips available to customers. Amazon shares climbed every trading day that week and gained around 9%, while the S&P 500 added about 1%.

But last year's lineup was arguably just as big. AWS made servers built with its next-generation Trainium3 chips available and unveiled its Nova 2 models, and the stock dipped about 2% in the conference week.

What does seem to move Amazon stock is its quarterly reports. Shares surged around 15% on July 31, the day after Amazon released its second-quarter results. In one day, the stock moved more than it did in any of those 10 post-conference months.

That makes sense, because AWS is where most of Amazon's profit comes from.

In the third quarter of 2026, AWS posted $17.8 billion in operating income, up 55% year over year. This was around 63% of Amazon's $28.1 billion in total operating income for the period.

Plus AWS's sales growth keeps accelerating. Its revenue rose 39% in the third quarter, faster than the second quarter's 37% and almost twice the 20% growth AWS had a year earlier.

Of course, all this takes heavy spending. Amazon still expects around $220 billion in cash capital expenditures in 2026, and that could weigh on its free cash flow for a while. And the stock isn't cheap, at about 25 times earnings based on what analysts expect for 2027. But with AWS growing almost twice as fast as it was a year ago, I think the price is fair.

So, is the month after re:Invent a good time to own Amazon stock? Going by the last 10 years, not especially. After all, four weeks is a short window, and I wouldn't use the record as a reason to rush in before the conference or to wait till after it.

I'd consider buying Amazon shares for the growth AWS keeps posting every quarter. I just wouldn't time my purchase around Nov. 30.

#3102. Crowd Pleaser….Crowdstrike Wants to Buy Zscaler

Here’s why this could be a game changer for cybersecurity investors.

By Randy Coon – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 9:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CrowdStrike ›

Two cybersecurity giants could be joining forces. But would a deal make CrowdStrike a better investment?

The cybersecurity industry may be on the verge of a blockbuster deal.

According to reports, CrowdStrike (CRWD -5.76%) is in talks to acquire Zscaler (ZS -5.29%), potentially bringing together two of the industry's most innovative companies in a transaction that could reshape the competitive landscape.

Investors reacted quickly sending Zscaler shares soaring roughly 17% in after-hours trading, while CrowdStrike shares fell about 8%.

For long-term investors, the question is what this combination could mean five or ten years from now.

CrowdStrike built its reputation protecting endpoints; the laptops, servers, and devices businesses rely on every day. Its Falcon platform uses artificial intelligence to identify and stop threats.

Zscaler approaches cybersecurity from another angle, pioneering zero-trust security that helps businesses safely connect employees to applications and data.

Put them together, and you potentially have something special: a comprehensive security platform protecting devices, users, networks, and cloud applications.

This is an incredibly powerful combo because cybersecurity customers increasingly want fewer vendors, simpler solutions, and better protection. And this combo creates an enormous opportunity.

First, cross-selling. CrowdStrike could introduce Zscaler's capabilities to its existing customers, while Zscaler could do the same with Falcon.

Second, competitive advantage. A combined company could become a more formidable competitor to Palo Alto Networks (PANW -5.09%) , strengthening its position in an increasingly consolidated industry.

Third, artificial intelligence. As businesses deploy more AI-powered applications and autonomous agents, protecting their digital infrastructure becomes increasingly complicated and increasingly essential.

Great acquisitions aren't automatically great investments. Zscaler carried a market capitalization of roughly $32 billion before the reports surfaced. That's a substantial price tag, and integrating two large technology platforms isn't easy.

CrowdStrike would also need to demonstrate that the benefits justify the acquisition premium, financing costs, and potential shareholder dilution.

Cybersecurity isn't a passing trend. It's becoming essential infrastructure for the digital economy. A CrowdStrike-Zscaler combination could create a powerhouse positioned to benefit from that transformation for years. But remember: A great business isn't always a great stock at any price.

Before getting excited, Foolish investors should watch the purchase price, financing structure, and management's plans for generating long-term value. Because ultimately, it's not the size of the deal that matters. It's the returns it delivers to shareholders. Both Zscaler and CrowdStrike are official Motley Fool recommendations. #3101. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3100. The S&P 500 Just Entered Correction Territory. Here's What It Means for Investors Right Now.

After plunging 8.6% for the week, the S&P 500 is now in a correction.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) just wrapped up one of its worst weeks in history, falling 8.6% as the AI trade collapsed. The index gave up 3.7% on Friday to close in correction territory, defined as a decline of 10% or more from a recent closing high. It was the first time the index had fallen below 7,000 since April.

Corrections are fairly routine events on the stock market. On average, the S&P 500 enters a correction about every 1.5 years, and its last correction came in March-April of 2025 as concerns about President Trump's tariffs and a trade war drove investors to sell.

Nervousness around the AI sector has been pushing tech stocks lower since the beginning of October, but the negative sentiment didn't really hit the S&P 500 until this week.

Investors are worried about rising interest rates squeezing the AI infrastructure build-out, including in the unprofitable neocloud sector; the backlash against data centers following the midterm elections; and the crisis unfolding at Nvidia after Jensen Huang resigned, with investigations into chip exports to China and now an accounting investigation by the SEC.

Fears of an AI bubble have been swirling in the market for a while, but despite some of the air coming out of these stocks, the fundamentals still look strong and valuations remain cheap.

Of the last 10 corrections in the S&P 500, which go back to 2011, the average decline has been 17%, and two of those turned into bear markets. That happened during the COVID crash early in 2020 and in 2022, a bear market that lasted most of the year.

The average duration from peak to a new peak was 9.4 months, and the average duration from the bottom to a new peak was 6.1 months, though the last two corrections took less than 100 days.

Regardless of the length of time, the S&P 500 has recovered from every correction it's experienced.

A correction is no reason to panic, and it's a regular part of stock market volatility. However, this is a good time to put together a watch list of stocks you'd like to buy if you have cash available. If you are a net buyer of stocks, a sell-off is actually a good thing, as Warren Buffett advised, as it makes stocks cheaper to buy.

Some quality stocks like Alphabet and Meta now have price-to-earnings ratios below 20, making them look attractive, and we're likely to see more deals if stocks fall further.

If you're not in a position to buy stocks, it's worth remembering that historically, the S&P 500 returns to an all-time high within nine months on average. How this sell-off plays out will depend on the AI sector, but the low valuations for many of the top AI stocks are an encouraging sign.
