#3015. SpaceX’s subsidiary Cursor tells lawmakers it's considering phasing out Chinese AI models

SpaceX's artificial-intelligence coding platform, Cursor, is considering moving away from using of Chinese AI models due to "geopolitical risk," CNBC has learned.

Cursor, which SpaceX acquired at an implied $60 billion equity value in August, had built its coding model Composer 2 on top of Chinese lab Moonshot AI's Kimi K2.5.

It's among the U.S. firms that have adopted Chinese models, whose usage has spiked across the globe this year, worrying lawmakers in Washington. The battle for AI supremacy between the U.S. and China has reached new heights in 2026 as Chinese models have improved.

But now Cursor plans to "explore phasing out PRC [People's Republic of China]-developed open-weight models because of geopolitical risk," according to a report produced by the House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China, shared exclusively with CNBC ahead of its publication on Friday.

The report, which follows a months-long investigation into the adoption of Chinese AI models into the American tech stack, said Cursor "informed the Committees" of those plans. Airbnb and DoorDash also engaged with the investigation about their use of Chinese AI models.

SpaceX announced it entered into a formal agreement to buy Cursor in June; the deal was completed in August. SpaceX is developing the Grok family of models, which Cursor has been involved in training.

Cursor, SpaceX and DoorDash did not respond to a CNBC request for comment. Airbnb declined to comment. The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The report found that cost, customization and near-frontier performance of Chinese AI models were driving adoption, with the U.S. lacking a competitive open-weight alternative at "price and performance the market demands."

"Beijing is now seeking to turn those technological gains into global market share and long-term dependence," the report says. China's embassy in the U.K. did not respond to a request for comment. China's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond.

It also sets out recommendations to address the issues it has identified.

Those include launching a "national American open-weight initiative" that combines public computing power, shared evaluation infrastructure and "lawful access" to American frontier models for training.

The report also says an order, made public in October, that banned DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems, should be built on, by prohibiting the use of "PRC-developed AI models" in federal information systems and in performance of federal contracts.

Separately, Washington is also concerned about Chinese companies accessing Nvidia chips remotely, via overseas data centers, and gaining ground by using "distillation," where new models use older, more established ones to help train them.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

#3013. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#3011. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#3010. U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returns from Asia

SHENZHEN, China — Even as Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation leaders gathered for high-profile photos at the summit's close, key issues from Nvidia to Taiwan remained unaddressed.

"The intrigue is around not what was said, but what remains unresolved," said Gary Dvorchak, managing director at Blueshirt.

"Taiwan remains a major source of strategic uncertainty. North Korea was notably absent from the trilateral nuclear declaration," he said. "Southeast Asia, which is increasingly central to global manufacturing and investment, was overshadowed by major-power diplomacy."

President Donald Trump did not meet with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un, despite speculation.

Taiwan, one of the 21 member economies of APEC, sent Acer Group founder and honorary chairman Stan Shih to Shenzhen. But the self-governed island, which Beijing claims is part of its territory, did not feature in any significant way during the event. Taiwan is set to hold key elections for Taipei mayor and other posts on Nov. 28.

Instead, the highlight of this week's APEC summit in Shenzhen was a trilateral meeting Thursday between Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. The three leaders, meeting together for the first time, reaffirmed interests in reducing nuclear risks and plans to increase oil production.

"The optics are impressive, but there's not much happening," Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group, said Friday of the trilateral on CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Resolving the years-long Ukraine war would require involvement from President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and European allies, Bremmer said.

Ahead of the trilateral, Trump and Xi also held their third bilateral summit of the year. Xi greeted Trump at the airport late Wednesday, reciprocating the hospitality shown in September when the Chinese leader visited Washington.

But little was shared publicly on whether the two countries agreed to reduce tariffs further, allow rare earths to keep flowing beyond a January deadline, or address tech competition.

Recent reports tying Jensen Huang to an alleged Chinese spy led to the executive's resignation as Nvidia CEO. It also raised questions in Congress about the effectiveness of U.S. efforts to restrict China's access to advanced chips used to train artificial intelligence.

Trump indicated to Fox News that Nvidia came up briefly in his talks with Xi. U.S. officials last week said Trump did not plan to raise the matter, while noting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk would accompany the U.S. president.

No other executive was named. Other American business leaders, including Visa CEO Ryan McInerney, who also chairs the U.S.-China Business Council, joined the APEC CEO Summit and was seen at a welcome banquet Wednesday evening.

However, Musk did not appear with Trump at major APEC-related events. CNBC was present for the opening portions of a banquet Wednesday and the Economic Leaders' Meeting Thursday and did not see Musk.

In fact, Musk flew to Shanghai Thursday morning to visit Tesla's factory there, a company executive said on Chinese social media. Musk also showed up in a social media video with Ren Hongbin, chairman of the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade.

Musk addressed Wednesday's DealBook Summit in New York via video conference, having previously said on X that he would be joining from China.

Tesla and the White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

"We still don't have a durable resolution of the U.S.-China trade dispute," Dvorchak said, adding: "Business needs certainty, not just ceremony."

Trump visited Shenzhen after briefly traveling to the Philippines on Tuesday for a meeting with the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping happening on the sidelines of the ASEAN Summit. Trump also met the prime minister of Thailand, Anutin Charnvirakul.

Topics of discussion included U.S. cooperation with the region, trade and critical minerals, U.S. Ambassador to ASEAN Kevin Kim told reporters Wednesday. He declined to comment on whether China's influence in the region was addressed.

Kim emphasized it was the second year in a row that Trump visited Southeast Asia, and noted the region "will be at the heart of SI [artificial intelligence] going forward," making it important that American technology is "getting into the right hands" through a regulatory framework the president is building.

Speaking to reporters a day later in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi also highlighted the ASEAN and Quad events over APEC. "The major outcome of this diplomatic week was the Quad Summit," she said, noting via official English translation how the four leaders expressed "strong opposition to the unilateral approach to change the status quo by force or coercion ... [as well as] arbitrary export control."

Takaichi, the first Japanese leader in seven years to visit China, received a cool reception upon her arrival in Shenzhen for the APEC meeting.

Tensions between Japan and China have escalated since Takaichi drew Beijing's ire in Nov. 2025 when she said that a Chinese attack on Taiwan could draw a Japanese military response.

Takaichi said Thursday that Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communiqué.

The communique said Japan "understands and respects" the People's Republic of China's position on Taiwan, but does not explicitly acknowledge Taiwan as part of the PRC.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#3007. Bitcoin suffered identical sell-off pattern ahead of Anthropic, SpaceX IPOs — what happens next?

Bitcoin has suffered nearly identical sell-offs ahead of two of the biggest public offerings this year, leaving investors wondering if competing demand for investor capital could be weighing on the cryptocurrency and perhaps delaying its recovery.

The price of the bitcoin dropped 22.7% in the 30 days before Anthropic's IPO and fell 22.3% in the month leading up to SpaceX's June 12 listing. Ether declined 25.3% in the 30 days preceding Anthropic's debut, and 28% in the same window ahead of SpaceX.

"Liquidity events can elevate correlations across asset classes quickly," Matthew Sheffield, chief investment officer at the ether treasury firm Sharplink, told CNBC. "The first 15 min of [Thursday's] Anthropic IPO put that on full display. Tech stocks, value stocks, oil, gold, crypto all sold off as traders sold assets to buy shares in the secondary market."

"Stores of value function as such over long horizons, but in the short term are still liquidity driven," Sheffield said. "We saw this in early Covid when the market fled to cash. Stock, bonds, commodities ... it didn't matter, you were being sold for cash."

While investors appear to be rotating capital out of crypto to make room for history-making IPO investments, that can't be said with precise certainty. Crypto has also been under pressure from ETF outflows, leverage, macro uncertainty and broader risk-off sentiment.

Still, the post-IPO price action may offer a data point in understanding where bitcoin's price goes next. With Anthropic now public, investors are watching to see whether bitcoin and ether find a floor, and if institutional demand returns.

"[After] the SpaceX IPO, bitcoin didn't truly find its final bottom until after the IPO was completed, and it didn't actually reverse the downtrend until 70 days later," said Matt Cole, CEO and chairman of Strive Asset Management, a bitcoin treasury firm. "My view is that [for] bitcoin, the most likely scenario is that it chops around here."

The expiration of IPO allocation restrictions can influence the timing of capital flows into digital assets, Sheffield said.

"Post SpaceX we saw the wealth effect in full force," Sheffield said. "Many brokers make traders wait 15-30 days before they are allowed to flip shares they received as allocations. … If you went long ETH versus SpaceX on the day of the IPO, on day 31 was when you would have become profitable."

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen. On Friday, it was sitting at about $62,000.

As of Friday, bitcoin was hovering at the upper end of a critical $60,000 to $62,000 support range. Its next move could determine whether it can stabilize or if it will face another leg lower and revisit its July low of $57,737.35.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from a combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin is in the early stages of forming a bottom, according to Cole. Retail fear is elevated amid recent outages, quantum concerns and other short-term uncertainties, but bitcoin should continue to benefit from macro forces like rising debt and weakening fiat currencies, which should drive price higher, he said.

"Capital markets are open, … retail fears are high, and when you combine those things it's much more likely to be a dip-buying opportunity across the board, including bitcoin, than the start of a great financial crisis," said Cole. "It'll likely age as a volatile time to consider allocating to risk, not the opposite."

"The winter for bitcoin is basically at the end, but after winter does not come summer, it comes early spring," he added. "Anytime anyone's been around early spring, what you notice is it gets really cold still, it still rains – it's not necessarily great weather, and I think that's the environment we're in for bitcoin."

#3004. Graves leaning on Raptors motto ‘Stronger Together’ amid losing streak

The Toronto Raptors are in a difficult spot.

While it may (or may not) be anything, there's an uneasy dread that surrounds Kawhi Leonard's injury timeline. Many fans anticipated going into the season understanding that Leonard's health would always loom over the team, but no one could have expected the string of injuries that have plagued the rest of the roster.

With RJ Barrett out for at least two weeks (hamstring), Scottie Barnes managing a groin injury (but expected to return), and Collin Murray-Boyles in concussion protocol, it's been a challenging start to the 2026-27 season.

And it doesn't get easier when the Raptors play the Detroit Pistons on Friday.

Here are two players to watch ahead of the 7:30 p.m. EST scheduled tip-off on Prime Video.

After playing a career-high 31 minutes against the Washington Wizards, Allen Graves took the podium to discuss the team's sixth consecutive loss. The analytics show why the 20-year-old is an intriguing player, but hearing him speak, it's always easy to see why the front office was enamoured with Graves the person.

Graves referred to one of the team's mottos, which can be found inside the OVO Athletic Centre: Stronger Together

"We're really taking emphasis on that and pulling together throughout this time because we've got each other," said Graves. "Trying to pull together so we can get out of this."

While the fix doesn't begin with Graves, the rookie plays an integral role in finding a solution to the team's woes, particularly with Murray-Boyles navigating a head injury.

After shooting 41.3 per cent on 2.6 attempts at Santa Clara, the shooting is finally starting to come around for Graves. He's still only averaging 28.1 per cent on 2.1 attempts through 15 games, but Graves is also confidently shooting 37.5 per cent on 2.6 attempts in the last six contests.

Highly regarded for his quick hands and activity on defence, Graves is taking the journey that every rookie goes on as they navigate the pro game's speed and officiating. While Graves had one steal and three blocks in the home opener against the Chicago Bulls on Oct. 21, he hasn't finished with at least 2+ stocks since. The only other time Graves has finished with at least one block and a steal was against the Minnesota Timberwolves on Oct. 23.

"Getting more comfortable playing on the ball and off the ball," said Graves. "Learning how to guard pin downs and guard screens."

Graves is a naturally gifted rebounder and it's only a matter of time before his elite timing transfers to his closeouts and decision-making on defence. It certainly accelerates the learning process when playing the New York Knicks in a doubleheader followed by the Pistons.

"You can tell they were a championship team," said Graves. "When they make mistakes, getting right back to it, next-play mentality, I feel like that's something we're growing in."

A breakout is happening in Detroit and it's being authored by one of the most intriguing players in the NBA – Ausar Thompson.

The do-it-all wing is averaging 10.4 points, 8.5 rebounds, 5.5 assists, 1.7 steals and 0.8 blocks in his fourth year in the league. Thompson's points, rebounds, and assists are all career-high averages. The fun part is that he's doing it while playing just three more minutes a game.

For those who play Yahoo Fantasy Basketball, Thompson likely made a tremendous impact in both category and points leagues over the last seven days.

vs. New Orleans: 10.0 points, 7.0 rebounds, 4.0 assists, 2.0 steals

vs. San Antonio: 14.0 points, 9.0 rebounds, 12.0 assists, 5.0 steals

vs. Miami: 10.0 points, 8.0 rebounds, 5.0 assists, 2.0 steals, 2.0 blocks

vs. Washington: 17.0 points, 11.0 rebounds, 11.0 assists, 3.0 steals, 3.0 blocks

Teams continue to famously scheme to avoid Thompson like he's a blanket cornerback, but now his numbers and usage are starting to reflect his sky-high potential.

#3003. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#3000. A wave of 'pent-up quits' could boost turnover in the job market, expert says: 'The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring'

Frustrated workers could trigger a wave of turnover, creating a shift in the sluggish job market, according to experts at the job boards Indeed and Glassdoor.

That sluggishness, or what labor economists call a low-hire, low-fire economy, means that fewer people are getting hired, and fewer workers are leaving their jobs — even as a growing share of job holders report frustration with their career opportunities. In 2026, 21% of workers report feeling dissatisfied with their career prospects, according to Glassdoor reviewer data. Dissatisfied workers rated their jobs at a one or two out of five stars.

A stagnant job market can discourage even the unhappiest of workers from quitting, but hiring could soon ramp up. Indeed's Job Posting Index, or JPI, which tracks the level of job postings on Indeed relative to a pre-pandemic baseline, saw year-over-year growth for the first time in September 2026 since 2022. The upward trend suggests that the job market is "heating up," Glassdoor chief economist Daniel Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

As more opportunities open up, job holders might regain the confidence to exit, turning into "a wave of quits and turnover," Zhao says. Turnover could also help those who are out of a job secure employment as roles that were once filled become vacant.

"The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring right now," Zhao said at a press event Tuesday in New York City. "There's a lot of pent-up potential energy, and it's unclear exactly which way that that spring is going to release that energy."

In a healthy job market, employee dissatisfaction can forecast turnover rates. When employee dissatisfaction reached 17% in April 2022, per Glassdoor reviews, quit rates also reached their highest levels at 3%, seasonally adjusted, according to data from Bureau of Labor Statistics Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey data published by the St. Louis Federal Reserve.

However, when workers feel anxious about their future job prospects, turnover can stall. Though workers feel dissatisfied with their jobs at higher rates in September 2026 than they did in April 2022, only 2.1% of workers quit their job in the month, according to the BLS JOLTS.

"There aren't those opportunities to climb the career ranks internally or externally," Zhao says of the job market. "There's no outlet for the frustration or dissatisfaction that [workers] might be feeling."

Employee turnover was one focus of Indeed and Glassdoor's 2027 Hiring and Worklife Trends Report, published Wednesday. Their research, which assumed a consistent relationship between worker dissatisfaction and resignations based on historical data, found that the quit rate is 0.22% lower than what employee sentiment would typically suggest. While the figure seems small, it is equivalent to an additional 349,000 quits per month.

"Workers are very hesitant to quit, especially without something lined up, because hiring is very sluggish, and long-term unemployment is looming over them," Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

While unemployment levels are stable, people are remaining jobless for longer, according to data from the BLS. Those who face long-term unemployment, job seekers who have been out of work for 27 weeks or more, accounted for 27.6% of all unemployed people in October 2026.

Even if more jobs open, turnover across sectors could vary. Industries that are highly exposed to artificial intelligence like software development, engineering and banking previously dragged Indeed's JPI down, meaning employers in these sectors posted fewer jobs. Indeed's data shows that postings in these sectors have since seen growth, and could result in what experts call "pent-up quits," which occur when dissatisfied workers leave their jobs once market conditions improve. Job posts on Indeed for employers in industries with less AI exposure like healthcare have softened, according to experts.

However, business and professional services, which includes banking, shed 27,000 jobs in October 2026, according to data from ADP Research.

"As we look ahead to 2027, that increase in demand might not necessarily translate into more hires, and it also might not be enough hires to give workers the confidence that they can actually quit their jobs and move on to something better," Zhao says.

Even as the job market appears to be trending in a positive direction, further economic pressure could continue to place a strain on employers and their hiring plans.

"Nobody was really expecting 2026 to come with the geopolitical challenges that have happened and the the impact that has had on energy prices and inflation," Zhao says. "And so there's always the outside chance that something happens in 2027 that derails the job market from the path that it's on."
